**Identity.** `llvmGetPassPluginInfo` returns plugin name `bennett` and version string
`dialect-0.2.12;isa-0.8`; the driver refuses a plugin whose string differs. [deps: DLT-19.9, DLT-23] One plugin build per LLVM
major (NS §3.2).

**Outputs** (in `-bennett-out`, file names plugin-defined, §24 D22): the materialised device
variants as bitcode (§17.7); the host module with device entries replaced by launch stubs; the ISA
objects (ISA §9, §16); the certificate (ISA §12) with `producer.materialisation = {llvm_version,
pipeline, normaliser_version: "0.2.8"}` and `bindings.dialect_mapping_version = "0.2.9"`; the coverage
report (ISA §13), whose `source` field is `dialect-0.2.12:<rule id>` (e.g. `dialect-0.2.12:N33`); the
blocker reports (§25.1); and the **build record**, one JCS object with the build flags, the
budget table (resolved, including each shape's selection_identity, final semantic identity,
resolved components and frozen layout capacities under §9.2) and its SHA-256, the runtimes, arith and asm-table hashes, the snapshot
hash, the layout options and the `cpuid` leaf table (§15.2). Its SHA-256 is recorded as the build metadata of the certificate's
`producer.version` (`<driver version>+build.<hex>`) until the ISA certificate has a field for it
(§25.2). [deps: DLT-19.9, DLT-25.2]

**A missing plugin fails loud.** No production artefact defines a marker: `bennett-runtimes`,
`bennett-arith` and the driver's link inputs define no `__bennett_*` marker symbol (test runtimes
such as the trace runtime do, and are never linked into production). A link in which the plugin
did not run therefore leaves undefined references and fails at link time; the driver MUST NOT
ignore unresolved symbols or supply weak marker definitions.

**Module flag.** A producer MAY add `!{i32 1, !"bennett.dialect", !"0.2.12"}` (behaviour Error, so an
LTO link of modules of two dialect versions fails); the plugin MUST reject a module whose flag
differs from its version (`BD-E-VERSION`). [deps: DLT-19.9, DLT-23]

```llvm example=version-flag verdict=reject:BD-E-VERSION
define void @top() {
  %h = call ptr @__bennett_qalloc(i64 0, i64 1)
  call void @__bennett_qfree(ptr %h)
  ret void
}

!llvm.module.flags = !{!0}
!0 = !{i32 1, !"bennett.dialect", !"0.1"}
```

## 20. Platform profile `riscv64-bennett-virt` (platform 0.2.1; rungs 6–7)

### 7.3 Region identity (maintainer decision 1)

A region is identified by its content, never by its symbol. Candidates that resolve to the same
function are one region. The semantic identity of an **oracle region** in a shape (§8.4) is the
interface hash (ISA §9.2) of `@ref` in its reference object (§16.2): every lproc, data image and
layout leaf of the closure is part of it, and `@ref`, `@L<n>`, `@G<n>` are fixed names, so two
functions with equal materialised bodies have equal identity whatever their source names. Symbol
names, linkage, aliases and thunks are non-normative: diagnostics and coverage entries MAY print
them. Budget lookup uses the separate provisional `selection_identity` and the
once-only staging of §9.2, never a fixed point in this final semantic identity.

The **region key** of any region is the SHA-256 of the text printed by the LLVM printer of the
major in use for a module that contains the materialised region function and its closure, after
`strip` (local names), with the functions renamed `f0, f1, …` in depth-first preorder from the
region along direct calls in instruction order, and without datalayout, triple and module
metadata. It keys the budget table (§9.2) and is recorded in the certificate and coverage
entries. It depends on the LLVM major, which the certificate records (§24, D13).

### 7.4 Kinds by content
### 17.7 The native comparison build

Differential testing (NS §5.1) compares the artefact with the **native comparison binary**: the
materialised device variants, code-generated by the same LLVM major and linked with
`bennett-runtimes` and `bennett-arith` (never host libc or libm) under the comparison link of
§19.5. It is never an `-O0` build: optimisation legally changes marker operands (spike F4), and
the materialised optimised module is the one program both builds implement. The driver MUST make
division by zero and INT_MIN/−1 trap in the native build: on targets whose divide instruction does
not trap it inserts, after materialisation and outside the materialised module, a check that
calls `__bennett_nz_fault` with code 5 or 6. Results compare by the outcome table of §18.7.

**Comparison-only support exception.** Only after the materialised device module has been fixed, the native comparison backend MAY define the three `__bennett_nz_` primitives of §3.1 as native support, and the driver MUST emit `__bennett_rt_native_layout` as specified in §19.5; these definitions MUST NOT enter producer inputs, production device bitcode, region identity or the materialised module used by the ISA map.

The native primitive support MUST reproduce the resolved device addresses, zero fill, live allocation sizes, nonreclaiming allocation/free history and fault outcomes of §§17.1/17.5, with capacity for every history admitted by the resolved heap budget (at most one heap record per byte since max(n,1) ≥ 1), without an arbitrary smaller record cap; host setup/resource failure MUST terminate comparison with an explicit infrastructure failure, never silently relocate the heap, choose defaults, use host malloc, or report a successful dialect outcome.

**Definedness classifiers.** The comparison binary MUST detect the materialised memory faults of §17.5 (out-of-live-allocation access, use after free and invalid/double free), division by zero and signed division overflow, and reached explicit fault/unreachable paths; instrumentation or a checked memory adapter may supply these checks outside the materialised module, and UBSan-only coverage MUST NOT be reported as satisfying the memory-fault obligation.

The harness MUST separately classify source inputs using instrumented original-input builds or a checked reference interpreter as in NS §3.2, report flagged inputs as undefined/excluded, and record the classifier's actual detection coverage; no classifier claims source UB erased by optimisation is detectable in the comparison binary.

# Mixed fat object — shared contract scope

Proposal for every revision in this directory; not a published ISA amendment.
Clause references are to [ISA 0.9](../../../spec/Bennett-ISA-Spec-0.9.md),
[the North Star](../../../../bennett/docs/Bennett-NorthStar.md), and, where
qualified, the maintained dialect 0.2/0.3 texts. This is the shared statement
required by bennett-4ht and the council rubric's standing mixed-fat-object check.

## Citable scoping statement

> A mixed fat object retains an ordinary native host binary and embeds ISA
> objects for marked device modules and their linked device closures (North
> Star §§2, 3.3, 4.4). ISA §§9.1–9.3 identify each device object and selected
> root; §12.3 certificates, owner records and reports cover that closure,
> with a separate binding/context set per device root and [] relative to
> that root (§12.1). Native entry, argv/envp and host-only libc need no ISA
> region, frame or certificate record; native comparison binds the selected
> materialised closure, not the whole host. Today §10 launches a device CC
> entry with copied arguments/entropy; §5.4 budgets bind device invocations,
> §3.3/§10 handles belong to a loaded program/run, and §7.3/§10 tapes cover
> device R effects. These require explicit container-to-object/root/certificate
> association, host-entry-to-device-entry bindings, budget/handle lifetime
> negotiation and tape/run association in a versioned mixed-launch contract.
> ISA §7.1 defines device memory but §10 defines no general host-memory-by-reference
> launch ABI: that contract must add address/layout mapping, range/liveness,
> aliasing, ownership/synchronisation, writeback and reversal rules, preserving
> dialect §§6.6, 8.5, 19.4–19.5. Until published, these are explicit
> bennett-4ht boundary obligations, not grounds to require whole-binary
> lowering or omit mixed execution.

## Present assumptions and exact publication changes

| Contract object | Assumption today | Exact change needed for mixed use |
|---|---|---|
| Object and linking (§§9.1–9.3) | One ISA object's closed sections and linked symbols; no native-container schema. | Publish a versioned inventory associating embedded/linked ISA object bytes and object_hash with selected device roots and certificates. A native-container hash, if used, is a separate identity. |
| Certificates/inventory (§§12.1–12.3) | Closure-keyed device-region records; current text does not explicitly delimit native inventory. | Insert the shared scoping rule in §12.3, cross-reference from §9.2, and require complete device-owner/link-proof coverage per root. Multiple device roots can share static closures without merging root-relative contexts. |
| Launch/entry (§10) | bennett_run(program, cc_entry, args, entropy) starts device CC; it is not native main. | Publish a host call/return shim and container/root selection/feature negotiation. Define native argument/result marshalling, including ptr (whose host binding §10 does not specify), status/fault propagation, suspend/reverse/close and ownership. Do not invent a current general launch shim. |
| Budgets (§5.4; §§10–11) | Validated token tuples and identity scoped to a program/run; action_call has no primary token (§4.7). | Bind selected device invocation budgets and token equality to the launch request; charge actual device/replay work. State host/shim accounting separately; a native process has no invented global B. |
| Handles (§3.3; §10; dialect §6.6) | Register ids refer to the loaded program's ledger; run ownership and token validity are explicit. | Map host-visible opaque handles to that program/ledger with validity and cross-launch ownership checks; preserve class vectors and token identities. Do not serialize native pointers as device ids or token tuples as identities. |
| Effect tape (§7.3; §10) | Run-associated R effects and explicit copied hostcall requests; no implicit log of native host I/O. | Associate each device run and tape with the launched root and define host effect/proxy ordering and replay at the boundary, preserving §7.4 reversal. Saved-journal interchange needs its own versioned replay profile. |
| Host memory by reference (§§7.1–7.2; dialect §§8.5, 17.1, 19.4–19.5) | Device layout plus dialect snapshot/replay and boundary-address/promotion rules; copied hostcall bytes do not provide general shared pointers. | Publish the mapping and lifetime protocol named in the statement, including allocator metadata/history and pointer-rich layouts, so ordinary host memory can be supplied safely without universal host promotion. Retain existing snapshot/replay as a construction, not the sole eventual capability. |

For example native main can call marked roots A and B in distinct ISA objects.
Each has its own object/root certificate and root context []; neither
certificate inventories main or host-only libc. A shared linked device
callee is checked in each root's actual invocation tree. A native comparison
executes the corresponding materialised region/callee closure under its
recorded layout, input and tool configuration (dialect §§17.7, 19.5, 19.9).

These notes specify boundary scope and exact remaining contract work; they
implement no container, host shim or memory registration. The existing
bennett-4ht issue tracks that publication/implementation obligation. Current
device contracts and their complete estimate/proof requirements remain in
force; an unimplemented boundary is not evidence that mixed execution works.

## 9. Objects, identity, linking, reversible ABI (object 0.9)

### 9.1 Sections and schemas

Order fixed: `symbols imports functions data devices layout schedule
certificate coverage`; each at most once; unknown sections or fields are
errors. Metadata sections are one JCS line each, an object:

```
  symbols:  { "<local index>": { "name", "kind": "cc"|"r"|"oracle"|"action"|"perm_action"|"lproc"|"data", "signature": <sig>, "interface_hash" } }
  imports:  { "<name>": { "signature": <sig>, "pinned_closure_hash"?: hex } }
  devices:  { "models": { "<dev>": { "version": "0.9" } }, "services": { "<reverse-dns id>": { "version": "…" } } }
  layout:   { "ptr_width", "H", "A_tab", "S_cells", "null_size", "heap_base", "heap_limit_max",
              "globals": { "<name>": { "base", "size", "image": "<data name>" } },
              "interface_regions": { "<oracle>": { "<region>": { "base" } } }, "block_image"?: "<data name>" }
  schedule: §11      certificate: §12      coverage: { "entries": [ §13 ] }
  <sig>:    kind "oracle": { "tier":"q", "kind", "in":[w…], "tgt":w, "fault":F, "mem":[{"A","W","role"}], "args":[types], "abi":"0.9" }
            kind "action"/"perm_action": { "tier":"q", "kind", "mut":[w…], "in":[w…], "mem":[…], "args":[types], "bounds":[…], "abi" }
            kind "lproc": { "tier":"q", "kind", "params":[w…], "result":w, "tokens":["token"…], "args":[types], "mem":[{"A","W","role":"in"}], "abi" }
            kind "cc"/"r": { "tier", "kind", "params":[types], "results":[types], "abi" }
            kind "data": { "tier":"-", "kind", "size", "abi" }
```

Integers that may exceed 2^53 are decimal strings, and a JSON number ≥
2^53 anywhere in metadata is an error. `data` lines are `NAME SP HEX`.
**Encodings.** `types` in `args`, `params` and `results` are the §16.2
type spellings as JSON strings (`"i64"`, `"ptr"`, `"f64"`, `"token"`,
`"qreg[8]"`); `w` in `in`, `tgt`, `mut`, lproc `params` and `result` is
the integer bundle or location width (§16.3). An lproc's `tokens`,
`args` and `mem` arrays are mandatory (empty arrays when absent from the
header); `args` excludes tokens and `mem` has only `in` roles (§3.7).
Array order is header order within each group. A **program-point id**
(§9.2) is the JSON array `[interface_hash, block name, ordinal]`.
Certificate `frame` fields use exactly the typed-record schema of §9.4;
unknown fields are errors there as in every metadata record.
`devices.models` lists every §7.3 model that an `effect` of the object
uses (an `effect` on an unlisted §7.3 model is a load error; a device
outside §7.3 is never listed and faults at run time, §17 item 3; a key
that is not a §7.3 model is an unknown field); `devices.services` lists
every `hostcall` service (§10). An interface region's name (its param
name and its `layout.interface_regions.<oracle>` key) MUST differ from
every symbol and import name of the object, since `@name` in `lift.addr`
denotes either (§9.2).

### 9.2 Identity

An lproc's token, classical and region parameter groups are part of
its JCS signature (§9.1), including their order and types. The header
and every forwarded operand list are canonical function bytes; changing
them changes the envelope and, through references, the caller's closure
hash. Fixed binding instances are derived per oracle (§3.7), not new
symbols or separately hashed bodies; instance rows share the source
row's program-point id and expand once per fixed row instance per step,
while the instance and semantic-call occurrence components in §11
invocation paths and §12.1 context keys distinguish their uses. [deps: ISA-3.7-P5, ISA-3.7-P6, ISA-11.1-P9, ISA-12.1-P11] Entry argument values and token tuples bind invocations through
`params_hash`, not through the lproc's interface hash (this section).
Imports must match all five groups; validation and instance formation
are repeated after linking (§9.3).

**Canonical function bytes**: the text from the function's header line
through its last instruction line, lines joined by LF, no trailing LF.
**Semantic envelope** of a symbol (byte-exact; every part ends with LF):

```
  (1) "isa=" <full version string, e.g. 0.9>
  (2) "ptr_width=" INT
  (3) the JCS signature
  (4) the canonical function bytes (with reference tokens replaced, below); for a data symbol: its data line NAME SP HEX
  (5a) for every data entry in the closure set, sorted by name bytewise:        "data " NAME " " HEX
  (5b) for every layout leaf in the closure set, sorted by JCS path bytes: "layout " <JCS key-component array> " " <JCS value>. [deps: ISA-9.1, ISA-16.1]
  (5c) for an R/CC function, for every device model and service it uses, sorted: "device " <name> " " <JCS entry>
  (6) for every referenced symbol outside the symbol's SCC, in order of first reference: its 64-hex hash
```

**Closure set**, by symbol kind. **Oracle**: **all** `layout` leaves of
the object except `interface_regions` entries of other oracles (every
global's pre-entry affects its allocation faults), its own `interface_
regions.<oracle>.*` leaves, and every data image those leaves name.
**Lproc**: the leaves and images of every global `@g` it names in `lift.
addr`. **Action / permutation action**: no layout leaves; the `@image`
of each `qregion` is an ordinary symbol reference (replaced in part (4),
listed in part (6)). **CC / R function**: all `layout` leaves, every
data image they name, and in (5c) every device model and `hostcall`
service it uses. **Data symbol**: parts (5)–(6) are empty. A layout leaf
is a scalar or an object at the deepest level the schema lists (`globals.
<name>` and `interface_regions.<oracle>.<region>` are leaves; `ptr_
width`, `H`, … are leaves); a scalar's JCS value is its decimal text.
**Globals are data symbols**: every global `g` in `layout.globals` MUST
have `image` = `g` and the object MUST define a `data` symbol named `g`
of that size, so the token `@g` in `lift.addr` is both the global and a
symbol reference (replaced by the data symbol's hash; the global's
`base` and `size` enter through the layout leaf). An `@name` naming an
interface region (in `lift.addr`) is not a symbol reference and is left
unchanged. **Reference replacement**: in parts
(4)–(5) every `@name` symbol-reference token is replaced by `@` followed
by the referenced symbol's 64-hex hash (the symbol's own name in its
header line is a definition, not a reference, and is left unchanged); the **interface hash** uses
interface hashes of defined symbols and `SHA-256(name ‖ 0x00 ‖ JCS
signature)` (hex) for unresolved imports; the **closure hash** uses
closure hashes. **Acyclic and cyclic symbols.** The reference graph
has an edge from a symbol to every symbol it references (parts
(4)–(5)). An **acyclic** symbol — a one-member SCC that does not
reference itself — has hash = SHA-256 of its own envelope (example 1's
`inc8`, example 2's `zero` and `sum_db`); the SCC procedure below applies
to every **cyclic** SCC, including a one-member SCC whose function
references itself (example 2's `sum`). **SCCs**: members ordered by name bytewise; every intra-
SCC `@name` token (parts (4)–(5)) is replaced by `@$<decimal index>`;
intra-SCC references contribute no part-(6) line; `scc_hash` = SHA-256
over the concatenation of the members' envelopes in that order; member
hash = SHA-256 over the ASCII bytes of `<scc_hash hex>:<decimal index>`.
**object_hash** = SHA-256 of the canonical object text with the
`schedule`, `certificate` and `coverage` sections deleted together with
the blank line preceding each. `reference_program_hash` = the oracle's
interface hash. `params_hash` = SHA-256 of the JCS array of classical
parameter values (the typed encoding of §12.3, K13); `schedule_hash` =
SHA-256 of the schedule's JCS bytes.
**Program-point id** = `(symbol interface hash, block name, instruction
ordinal within the block counting nested lines in text order)`.
Executable certificates and caches bind to **closure hashes** (required,
§12).

A layout leaf's path is the JCS array of its original JSON key
components from the layout root to the deepest schema-listed leaf,
so a scalar has a one-component path and an interface-region leaf has
path `["interface_regions","<oracle>","<region>"]`.
[deps: ISA-9.1-P1, ISA-16.1]
Part (5b) emits exactly one line for each structured leaf in the closure
set, including distinct leaves whose dotted renderings coincide, ordered
by the unsigned UTF-8 bytes of that JCS path array, independently of the
leaf values; no dotted rendering is used as a key or an ordering key.
[deps: ISA-9.1-P1, ISA-16.1]
An oracle's own interface-region leaves and entries of other oracles
are selected by equality of the original JSON key under
`interface_regions` to the oracle's name, never by a prefix of a
rendered path; an lproc's global leaves are selected by equality of
the key under `globals` to the global name.
[deps: ISA-3.7-P1, ISA-9.1-P1]

**Envelope boundary lemma** (non-normative).
For a function symbol, §16.2 requires a header beginning `fn `,
`oracle `, `action `, `perm_action ` or `lproc `; following lines are
`regs`, block labels, instructions, or structured closing braces. [deps: ISA-16.2]
No §4 opcode is `data`, `layout` or `device`; a result with one of
those names has ` = ` after the name, which cannot begin any part-(5)
line (a data name, a JSON array, or a device name is required there). [deps: ISA-4.8, ISA-9.1, ISA-16.2]
No canonical function line is a bare 64-hex string, so the first
part-(5)/(6) line ends the function bytes unambiguously. [deps: ISA-4.8, ISA-16.2]
The fixed `data `, `layout `, `device ` prefixes distinguish (5a–c),
and a bare 64-hex line distinguishes (6); JSON strings escape LF. [deps: ISA-9.1, ISA-16.1]
For a data symbol, the signature kind selects exactly one data line
for (4), with (5)–(6) empty, even when the data name itself is `data`,
`layout` or `device`. [deps: ISA-9.1, ISA-16.2]
Together with the fixed first three lines and the injective JCS array
paths, these facts make the envelope parts recoverable without length
prefixes; this is an encoding argument, not a claim of SHA-256 injectivity. [deps: ISA-9.1, ISA-16.1, ISA-16.2, ISA-9.2-P4]

### 9.3 Linking

Imports resolve by name + signature (mismatch: link error; pinned hash:
equality required); resolved closure hashes are recorded in the linked
certificate. Equal-closure-hash duplicates deduplicate; unequal under one
name, or conflicting `devices`/`layout`/`data` entries: link error.
Linking = resolution, validation, certificate composition (§12.3),
rebinding to closure hashes.

### 9.4 Reversible ABI

- CC/R calls: classical frame; callee frames follow the call boundary;
  `uncall` reverses segments (§7.4).
Constants are zero or X of a clean bit. All formulas use old source
values captured before an assignment/exchange. External effective
controls apply to the resulting semantic gates, not allocations; scratch
computations may be unconditional provided their compute/uncompute pair
is balanced when the external control is false (§3.5, §4.3).

| Family | Ordered expansion | Motivating 0.8 sentence |
|---|---|---|
| Copy, XOR, exchange; native gates | `qxor`: CX s[i] t[i]; `qcopy`: allocate then the same; `quncopy`: the same then release; `qexch`: SWAP a[i] b[i]. Keep each §4.4 gate as a leaf; target schemes expand later. | §4.5: "`cx s[i] t[i]`" and "`swap a[i] b[i]`"; §8.3: "Every template gate MUST itself match a gates entry". |
| Ripple add/sub and comparisons | c[0]=0; p[i]=a[i] XOR b[i]; sum[i]=p[i] XOR c[i]; c[i+1]=(a[i] AND b[i]) XOR (p[i] AND c[i]); compute each bit in this order, XOR sums out, reverse scratch. Subtraction adds bitwise NOT b with c[0]=1. Unsigned a<b is NOT the final subtraction carry; equality is the AND of NOT(a[i] XOR b[i]) in increasing-bit left-associated order. `ule` is OR(ult,eq); signed comparison selects sign(a) if signs differ, else ult. | §17 item 7: "ripple-carry adders and comparators"; §4.1: "wrap"; §3.7: prospective sums use "no wrap" (retain the extra carry for those). |
| Other arithmetic, casts, select | AND/XOR/OR use the Boolean trees above; select emits ((NOT p) AND b[i]) XOR (p AND a[i]). Multiply folds shifted partial products in increasing multiplier-bit order through ripple add modulo 2^N. Shifts scan shift amounts j=0..N−1, equality-selecting the corresponding source bit; ≥N produces zero. Sign-extend replicates the high source bit, zero-extend supplies zero, truncate drops high bits. Division uses restoring unsigned long division, scanning dividend bits from N−1 to 0: append that bit to an N+1-bit remainder, compare with the zero-extended divisor, subtract iff ≥, and emit that predicate as quotient bit. Signed forms conditionally negate operands, divide magnitudes, negate quotient iff signs differ and remainder iff dividend negative; row fault predicates precede these outputs. | §4.2: "w (under ¬flag)" and the div fault predicates; §4.1 wrap/shift rules and signed MIN/−1 fault; §3.3 equal operand widths. |
| Linear inrange and flags | For table entries i=0..2^A_tab−1 compute `(i<atop) AND live_i AND (base_i≤p) AND (p+n≤base_i+size_i)` in that parenthesisation at §4.2's widened width; left-fold OR from false, then uncompute entry scratch. `free` and allocator/call fault trees use their explicit §4.2/§3.7 formula and slot order (including 0.9's E17.13 correction). | §4.2: "inrange(p,n) = ∃ i < atop ..." and "no wrap"; §17 item 7: "linear inrange scans". |
| Address dispatch and multiplexers | Scan every address i in increasing numeric order; compute equality to i, copy/exchange cell bits in increasing order under that bit, uncompute equality before the next i. Lifted memory chooses the §7.2 mapped region in address order and scans its cells in increasing address order; captures use old addresses before exchanged counters. | §4.6: "for each cell i: under a == i"; §17 item 7: "one-hot address dispatch"; §3.7 C1–C5/R1–R5. |
| Row, oracle and structure | Compose these recipes in the printed §3.6 step order, §4.2 slot order, §3.7 activation order, §5.2 allocate/forward/commit/reverse/release order. `qif`, loops, local-region image X gates and `qinplace` use the literal sequences of §4.5–§4.6, incorporating 0.9's explicit parameters. Cleanup `reverse` adjoints that sequence; `recompute` follows §6.4. | §11: "Above gate level the order is fixed by §3.6, §4.2, §4.5–§4.6 and §5.2"; §5.2: "run the inverse step for t = B..1, then release (1)". |

The permutation algorithms define exact permutation circuits, preserving
every input required by the row and cleaning scratch; circuit correctness must still be checked
against full-domain T3 witnesses and §5.7, not inferred from costs or
names. Complete catalog: **219 recipes**; envelope SHA-256
`5de4e4995350313e50217880ba1b95de39cd043283b726337d4894efb005d197`. [deps: ISA-11.1-P2, ISA-11.1-P3, ISA-11.1-P28, ISA-11.1-P29]
Publication follows acceptance. [deps: ISA-11.1-P1, ISA-11.1-P2] In particular this is
not permission to publish a partial library that omits division, stack,
coherent control, memory or any instruction.

**ISA-REF-LIB witness**

```json
{"isa":"0.9","name":"ref","recipes":[{"kind":"qxor","operations":[{"gate":"cx","ordinal":0,"wires":["s[0]","t[0]"]},{"gate":"cx","ordinal":1,"wires":["s[1]","t[1]"]}],"removable":[],"scratch":[],"source":"0.8 §4.5","width":2}],"version":"0.9"}
```

This is a single instantiated recipe witness, **not** a complete
library document for binding a schedule. Its envelope is the four-line
envelope above with this entire JSON as line four. It has two ordinals,
0 and 1, no scratch and no removable operations. For every two-bit s,t
(16 tuples), the result is `(s, t XOR s)`; reversing visits ordinal 1
then 0 and restores the entry tuple. Its hash identifies only these
witness bytes, not `ref@0.9` as a whole.

Witness envelope SHA-256: `299510fd67cc626fdcbcc10adcd40730f9af294ba596d86fb25fd31be08a5c7f`.

## 12. Certificate (cert 0.9; JCS)

### 12.1 Claims

| kind | payload | checker obligation |
|---|---|---|
| `exhaustive` | `domain` (§12.2); `expected`: `"oracle"` \| `{ "table": [...] }` \| `{ "program": closure_hash }` \| `"inverse_pair"` | re-execute every domain element on the reference interpreter / expected program; compare full tuples. For `"inverse_pair"` (the claim of an `inverse_pairs` entry, §12.3): for every element (x, ins, mem cells, args) run g and ginv at the entry's tuple, token-typed arguments at their `arg_tokens` tuples, and require both fault-free, ginv(g(x)) = x and g(ginv(x)) = x; the bundles and regions components MUST be the full domain of g's `in` and `mem` slots (the `args` component lists the covered classical values) |
| `sampled` | `domain`; `expected`: `"oracle"` \| `{ "table": [...] }` \| `{ "program": closure_hash }`; `subject` and invocation binding as below (K13); `sampler`: `{ "algorithm": "xoshiro256**", "seed": [4 decimal strings, not all zero], "count": n }` | independently replay both sides of the declared relation on every sampled element; compare full tuples; always **evidence**, even if every element is drawn |
| `structural` | `{ "rule": "translation_lemma" \| "inverse_pair_library" \| "adjoint_table", "subclaims": [indices], "witness": … }`; for `translation_lemma`: `{ "V": [points], "W": [points], "X": [wires], "B": [wires], "frame": [first point, last point] }`; for `inverse_pair_library`: `{ "library_closure_hash", "claim_index" }`; for `adjoint_table`: `{}` | check the rule's side conditions syntactically against the object and the subclaims' results |
| `numerical` | `{ "subject": { "closure_hash", "variant", "inherited_controls", "schedule_hash" }, "metric": "operator_norm", "precision_bits": n, "bound": decimal }` | **checker-computed, for every invocation context of the subject** (below): build the realised unitary U of that invocation (the region's realisation under the root schedule at that path, with its resolved classical arguments, budget, epsilon shares and cleanup choices, and `inherited_controls` control wires prepended) and the reference unitary V (the region's §5.3 semantics with the same arguments, controlled on the same wires) at the subject's signature widths in interval arithmetic at `precision_bits` (basis order = allocation order, controls first), form interval enclosures of `V†U − I` entrywise, derive the operator-norm bound as the Frobenius-norm upper bound in interval arithmetic, and verify it is ≤ `bound`; the claim holds iff it holds for every context |
| `channel` | `{ "subject": as above, "metric": "diamond", "precision_bits", "bound", "dual": [ { "Y": interval Hermitian matrix, "mu": decimal } … ] }` | for every invocation context of the subject, in enumeration order, verify its dual witness for the **diamond-norm SDP** below in interval arithmetic and `mu` ≤ `bound` (one witness per context; too few witnesses: reject) |

### 11.1 Reference-library contract (K11)

**ISA-REF-LIB** (K11; b-isa-spec-8r4, from bennett-vm-47t).
This is the decided 0.9 contract. The catalog bound as ref version 0.9
is the file `spec/ref-0.9.json` whose four-line envelope hashes to
`5de4e4995350313e50217880ba1b95de39cd043283b726337d4894efb005d197`.
It publishes the complete 219-recipe catalog, byte-identical to the
accepted catalog snapshot. Consumers lacking the bound library report
`E_SCHEDULE_INFEASIBLE` as §11 requires. The witness below is not a
full-library hash.

**Serialization and identity.** The schema and family witnesses are
documented in `spec/ref-0.9-draft.README.md`. The JSON file is RFC 8785 canonical
UTF-8, with no trailing LF, containing exactly `isa`, `name`, `version`
and `recipes`. The first three are strings `0.9`, `ref`, `0.9`.
`recipes` is an array in bytewise `kind` order, then increasing parameter
tuple order. Each instantiated recipe has `kind`, its integer width or
other named integer parameters, `operations`, `scratch`, `removable`
and `source` (the motivating ISA sentence). A parameterised recipe
uses `parameters` (an ordered array of formals defined below) and `body` instead of the
instance's `operations`; its body uses the closed constructors below.
Recipes cover every §4 instruction/row, the §3.6 step, §3.7 activation
operations and §5.2 construction, with no implementation-private leaf.
Classical instructions/effects remain classical leaf events (§8.2),
not invented quantum gates. The README documents schema, parameters,
coverage and witnesses; it is not hashed and cannot alter operations.

The library hash is SHA-256 of this UTF-8 envelope (every line ends LF,
including the last); the fourth line is the **entire** canonical library
JSON, including all recipes, sources and versions, without an embedded
hash field:

```
isa=0.9
expansion=ref
version=0.9
<JCS library document>
```

`bindings.expansion` names `ref`, version `0.9`, and this 64-lowercase-hex
hash. This adopts §9.2's version-prefixed, LF-terminated **envelope
convention**, with explicit expansion-domain and library-version lines;
it is a new library rule, not §9.2's symbol signature/SCC envelope.
Motivation (0.8 §11): "a versioned document, identified in the schedule's
`bindings.expansion` as { name, version, hash }"; 0.8 §9.2: "Semantic
envelope ... every part ends with LF". A changed recipe, source, scratch
name or ordering changes the library hash; no schedule/certificate can
silently inherit that change (§12.1's realised-subject binding).

**Expansion and ordinals.** Recipe bodies are ordered arrays
of constructors: a gate (`gate`, ordered `wires`, optional `params`), an
allocation (`alloc`, `width`, `class`, `state`), release (`release`),
classical event (`classical`, `point`), counted loop (`for`, `from`,
`to`, `body`), named recipe expansion (`invoke`, ordered `args`), or
inverse (`inverse`, `body`). Constructor objects have exactly those
keys. Fully instantiated `operations` contain only emitted leaves, each
also carrying its computed `ordinal`; unexpanded `body` constructors
carry no ordinal. Scalars are integer literals, parameter names, or prefix arrays
`[op, operands…]` for `+ - * div mod bit`; div is floor division with a
positive divisor. `bit(x,i)` requires nonnegative integers x and i and
returns floor(x/2^i) mod 2, with no fixed machine-word truncation (K23).
For x=13, bit(x,0..4) is 1,0,1,1,0; literal construction emits X only
for the one bits, in increasing bit order. Wires are declaration names with integer-expression
subscripts. Loops enumerate [from,to) in increasing order; expansion is
depth first, array order, and recipe dependencies are acyclic. An
inverse reverses its fully expanded body and adjoints each leaf by
§5.3, interchanging allocation/release. The complete serialized catalog
must encode the algorithms below in these constructors before release;
this document does not claim that prose alone is an interchangeable file.

**Typed binding (K19).** A formal is `{ "name": name, "type": type }`,
with exactly these keys, except `wire`, which additionally requires
`width`, and `templates`, which additionally requires `length` (integer
expressions using earlier integer formals); `arguments` requires `symbol`,
and `value` requires `isa_type`, as specified in K24; K25–K27 add
`records`, `region`, `class`, and `allocation` with the keys specified there. Types are `integer`,
`wire`, `angle`, `body`, `symbol`, `environment`, `event`, `image`,
`templates`, `arguments`, `value`, `records`, `region`, `class`, and
`allocation`. A bare name abbreviates an
`integer` formal, retaining the printed witness bytes. Names are unique
within a parameter list. Width expressions use only earlier integer
formals. A wire formal of width n is the ordered list w[n], with
little-endian indexing, including the empty list when n = 0.
| Other arithmetic, casts, select | AND/XOR/OR use the Boolean trees above; select emits ((NOT p) AND b[i]) XOR (p AND a[i]). Multiply folds shifted partial products in increasing multiplier-bit order through ripple add modulo 2^N. Shifts scan shift amounts j=0..N−1, equality-selecting the corresponding source bit; ≥N produces zero. Sign-extend replicates the high source bit, zero-extend supplies zero, truncate drops high bits. Division uses restoring unsigned long division, scanning dividend bits from N−1 to 0: append that bit to an N+1-bit remainder, compare with the zero-extended divisor, subtract iff ≥, and emit that predicate as quotient bit. Signed forms conditionally negate operands, divide magnitudes, negate quotient iff signs differ and remainder iff dividend negative; row fault predicates precede these outputs. | §4.2: "w (under ¬flag)" and the div fault predicates; §4.1 wrap/shift rules and signed MIN/−1 fault; §3.3 equal operand widths. |
| Linear inrange and flags | For table entries i=0..2^A_tab−1 compute `(i<atop) AND live_i AND (base_i≤p) AND (p+n≤base_i+size_i)` in that parenthesisation at §4.2's widened width; left-fold OR from false, then uncompute entry scratch. `free` and allocator/call fault trees use their explicit §4.2/§3.7 formula and slot order (including 0.9's E17.13 correction). | §4.2: "inrange(p,n) = ∃ i < atop ..." and "no wrap"; §17 item 7: "linear inrange scans". |
| Address dispatch and multiplexers | Scan every address i in increasing numeric order; compute equality to i, copy/exchange cell bits in increasing order under that bit, uncompute equality before the next i. Lifted memory chooses the §7.2 mapped region in address order and scans its cells in increasing address order; captures use old addresses before exchanged counters. | §4.6: "for each cell i: under a == i"; §17 item 7: "one-hot address dispatch"; §3.7 C1–C5/R1–R5. |
| Row, oracle and structure | Compose these recipes in the printed §3.6 step order, §4.2 slot order, §3.7 activation order, §5.2 allocate/forward/commit/reverse/release order. `qif`, loops, local-region image X gates and `qinplace` use the literal sequences of §4.5–§4.6, incorporating 0.9's explicit parameters. Cleanup `reverse` adjoints that sequence; `recompute` follows §6.4. | §11: "Above gate level the order is fixed by §3.6, §4.2, §4.5–§4.6 and §5.2"; §5.2: "run the inverse step for t = B..1, then release (1)". |

The permutation algorithms define exact permutation circuits, preserving
every input required by the row and cleaning scratch; circuit correctness must still be checked
against full-domain T3 witnesses and §5.7, not inferred from costs or
names. Complete catalog: **219 recipes**; envelope SHA-256
`5de4e4995350313e50217880ba1b95de39cd043283b726337d4894efb005d197`. [deps: ISA-11.1-P2, ISA-11.1-P3, ISA-11.1-P28, ISA-11.1-P29]
Publication follows acceptance. [deps: ISA-11.1-P1, ISA-11.1-P2] In particular this is
not permission to publish a partial library that omits division, stack,
coherent control, memory or any instruction.

**ISA-REF-LIB witness**

```json
{"isa":"0.9","name":"ref","recipes":[{"kind":"qxor","operations":[{"gate":"cx","ordinal":0,"wires":["s[0]","t[0]"]},{"gate":"cx","ordinal":1,"wires":["s[1]","t[1]"]}],"removable":[],"scratch":[],"source":"0.8 §4.5","width":2}],"version":"0.9"}
```

This is a single instantiated recipe witness, **not** a complete
library document for binding a schedule. Its envelope is the four-line
envelope above with this entire JSON as line four. It has two ordinals,
0 and 1, no scratch and no removable operations. For every two-bit s,t
(16 tuples), the result is `(s, t XOR s)`; reversing visits ordinal 1
then 0 and restores the entry tuple. Its hash identifies only these
witness bytes, not `ref@0.9` as a whole.

Witness envelope SHA-256: `299510fd67cc626fdcbcc10adcd40730f9af294ba596d86fb25fd31be08a5c7f`.

## 12. Certificate (cert 0.9; JCS)
through its last instruction line, lines joined by LF, no trailing LF.
**Semantic envelope** of a symbol (byte-exact; every part ends with LF):

```
  (1) "isa=" <full version string, e.g. 0.9>
  (2) "ptr_width=" INT
  (3) the JCS signature
  (4) the canonical function bytes (with reference tokens replaced, below); for a data symbol: its data line NAME SP HEX
  (5a) for every data entry in the closure set, sorted by name bytewise:        "data " NAME " " HEX
  (5b) for every layout leaf in the closure set, sorted by JCS path bytes: "layout " <JCS key-component array> " " <JCS value>. [deps: ISA-9.1, ISA-16.1]
  (5c) for an R/CC function, for every device model and service it uses, sorted: "device " <name> " " <JCS entry>
  (6) for every referenced symbol outside the symbol's SCC, in order of first reference: its 64-hex hash
```

**Closure set**, by symbol kind. **Oracle**: **all** `layout` leaves of
the object except `interface_regions` entries of other oracles (every
global's pre-entry affects its allocation faults), its own `interface_
regions.<oracle>.*` leaves, and every data image those leaves name.
**Lproc**: the leaves and images of every global `@g` it names in `lift.
addr`. **Action / permutation action**: no layout leaves; the `@image`
of each `qregion` is an ordinary symbol reference (replaced in part (4),
listed in part (6)). **CC / R function**: all `layout` leaves, every
data image they name, and in (5c) every device model and `hostcall`
service it uses. **Data symbol**: parts (5)–(6) are empty. A layout leaf
is a scalar or an object at the deepest level the schema lists (`globals.
<name>` and `interface_regions.<oracle>.<region>` are leaves; `ptr_
width`, `H`, … are leaves); a scalar's JCS value is its decimal text.
**Globals are data symbols**: every global `g` in `layout.globals` MUST
have `image` = `g` and the object MUST define a `data` symbol named `g`
of that size, so the token `@g` in `lift.addr` is both the global and a
symbol reference (replaced by the data symbol's hash; the global's
`base` and `size` enter through the layout leaf). An `@name` naming an
interface region (in `lift.addr`) is not a symbol reference and is left
unchanged. **Reference replacement**: in parts
(4)–(5) every `@name` symbol-reference token is replaced by `@` followed
by the referenced symbol's 64-hex hash (the symbol's own name in its
header line is a definition, not a reference, and is left unchanged); the **interface hash** uses
interface hashes of defined symbols and `SHA-256(name ‖ 0x00 ‖ JCS
signature)` (hex) for unresolved imports; the **closure hash** uses
closure hashes. **Acyclic and cyclic symbols.** The reference graph
has an edge from a symbol to every symbol it references (parts
(4)–(5)). An **acyclic** symbol — a one-member SCC that does not
reference itself — has hash = SHA-256 of its own envelope (example 1's
`inc8`, example 2's `zero` and `sum_db`); the SCC procedure below applies
to every **cyclic** SCC, including a one-member SCC whose function
references itself (example 2's `sum`). **SCCs**: members ordered by name bytewise; every intra-
SCC `@name` token (parts (4)–(5)) is replaced by `@$<decimal index>`;
intra-SCC references contribute no part-(6) line; `scc_hash` = SHA-256
over the concatenation of the members' envelopes in that order; member
hash = SHA-256 over the ASCII bytes of `<scc_hash hex>:<decimal index>`.
**object_hash** = SHA-256 of the canonical object text with the
`schedule`, `certificate` and `coverage` sections deleted together with
the blank line preceding each. `reference_program_hash` = the oracle's
interface hash. `params_hash` = SHA-256 of the JCS array of classical
parameter values (the typed encoding of §12.3, K13); `schedule_hash` =
SHA-256 of the schedule's JCS bytes.
**Program-point id** = `(symbol interface hash, block name, instruction
ordinal within the block counting nested lines in text order)`.
Executable certificates and caches bind to **closure hashes** (required,
§12).

A layout leaf's path is the JCS array of its original JSON key
components from the layout root to the deepest schema-listed leaf,
so a scalar has a one-component path and an interface-region leaf has
path `["interface_regions","<oracle>","<region>"]`.
[deps: ISA-9.1-P1, ISA-16.1]
Part (5b) emits exactly one line for each structured leaf in the closure
set, including distinct leaves whose dotted renderings coincide, ordered
by the unsigned UTF-8 bytes of that JCS path array, independently of the
leaf values; no dotted rendering is used as a key or an ordering key.
[deps: ISA-9.1-P1, ISA-16.1]
An oracle's own interface-region leaves and entries of other oracles
are selected by equality of the original JSON key under
`interface_regions` to the oracle's name, never by a prefix of a
rendered path; an lproc's global leaves are selected by equality of
the key under `globals` to the global name.
[deps: ISA-3.7-P1, ISA-9.1-P1]

**Envelope boundary lemma** (non-normative).
For a function symbol, §16.2 requires a header beginning `fn `,
`oracle `, `action `, `perm_action ` or `lproc `; following lines are
`regs`, block labels, instructions, or structured closing braces. [deps: ISA-16.2]
No §4 opcode is `data`, `layout` or `device`; a result with one of
those names has ` = ` after the name, which cannot begin any part-(5)
line (a data name, a JSON array, or a device name is required there). [deps: ISA-4.8, ISA-9.1, ISA-16.2]
No canonical function line is a bare 64-hex string, so the first
part-(5)/(6) line ends the function bytes unambiguously. [deps: ISA-4.8, ISA-16.2]
The fixed `data `, `layout `, `device ` prefixes distinguish (5a–c),
and a bare 64-hex line distinguishes (6); JSON strings escape LF. [deps: ISA-9.1, ISA-16.1]
For a data symbol, the signature kind selects exactly one data line
for (4), with (5)–(6) empty, even when the data name itself is `data`,
`layout` or `device`. [deps: ISA-9.1, ISA-16.2]
Together with the fixed first three lines and the injective JCS array
paths, these facts make the envelope parts recoverable without length
prefixes; this is an encoding argument, not a claim of SHA-256 injectivity. [deps: ISA-9.1, ISA-16.1, ISA-16.2, ISA-9.2-P4]

### 9.3 Linking

Imports resolve by name + signature (mismatch: link error; pinned hash:
equality required); resolved closure hashes are recorded in the linked
certificate. Equal-closure-hash duplicates deduplicate; unequal under one
name, or conflicting `devices`/`layout`/`data` entries: link error.
Linking = resolution, validation, certificate composition (§12.3),
rebinding to closure hashes.

## 16. The lifting map (map 0.2.9)

The lifting map turns a materialised region function, a binding shape (§8.4) and the link
layout (§19.5) into the **reference object** of that shape: the ISA object whose oracle `@ref`
defines F_B and the budget unit by the reference interpreter (ISA §4.2 "Reference body and
binding", §14.1). It is a function of its inputs alone, so two plugins on the same LLVM version
and pipeline produce byte-identical reference objects. Its version (`map 0.2.9`) is the
certificate's `dialect_mapping_version` (ISA §12.3). The plugin's device objects (§17.4) are
plugin-defined; they MUST be extensionally equal to the reference objects (ISA §5.7, T6; §18.7).

### 16.1 Liftable means materialised, always

Every materialised function is liftable (outline B1): §12 legalises every construct into integer
SSA values of at most 128 bits, memory, calls and branches at the map boundary.
Materialised LLVM may still contain bit-exact FP forms and aggregates; §16.4 maps these
through integer carriers and field locations. Every such accepted form has a row. 0.1's
`BD-E-LIFT-SUBSET` is deleted.

Every materialised function in an oracle closure MUST remain liftable through the complete normalisation and mapping tables, including memory, recursion, indirect calls, vectors and floating-point carriers. [deps: DLT-12.2, DLT-C, DLT-16.1-P1, DLT-16.4, ISA-3.7, ISA-4.2, ISA-7.2]

**Identity.** `llvmGetPassPluginInfo` returns plugin name `bennett` and version string
`dialect-0.2.12;isa-0.8`; the driver refuses a plugin whose string differs. [deps: DLT-19.9, DLT-23] One plugin build per LLVM
major (NS §3.2).

**Outputs** (in `-bennett-out`, file names plugin-defined, §24 D22): the materialised device
variants as bitcode (§17.7); the host module with device entries replaced by launch stubs; the ISA
objects (ISA §9, §16); the certificate (ISA §12) with `producer.materialisation = {llvm_version,
pipeline, normaliser_version: "0.2.8"}` and `bindings.dialect_mapping_version = "0.2.9"`; the coverage
report (ISA §13), whose `source` field is `dialect-0.2.12:<rule id>` (e.g. `dialect-0.2.12:N33`); the
blocker reports (§25.1); and the **build record**, one JCS object with the build flags, the
budget table (resolved, including each shape's selection_identity, final semantic identity,
resolved components and frozen layout capacities under §9.2) and its SHA-256, the runtimes, arith and asm-table hashes, the snapshot
hash, the layout options and the `cpuid` leaf table (§15.2). Its SHA-256 is recorded as the build metadata of the certificate's
`producer.version` (`<driver version>+build.<hex>`) until the ISA certificate has a field for it
(§25.2). [deps: DLT-19.9, DLT-25.2]

**A missing plugin fails loud.** No production artefact defines a marker: `bennett-runtimes`,
`bennett-arith` and the driver's link inputs define no `__bennett_*` marker symbol (test runtimes
such as the trace runtime do, and are never linked into production). A link in which the plugin
did not run therefore leaves undefined references and fails at link time; the driver MUST NOT
ignore unresolved symbols or supply weak marker definitions.

**Module flag.** A producer MAY add `!{i32 1, !"bennett.dialect", !"0.2.12"}` (behaviour Error, so an
LTO link of modules of two dialect versions fails); the plugin MUST reject a module whose flag
differs from its version (`BD-E-VERSION`). [deps: DLT-19.9, DLT-23]

```llvm example=version-flag verdict=reject:BD-E-VERSION
define void @top() {
  %h = call ptr @__bennett_qalloc(i64 0, i64 1)
  call void @__bennett_qfree(ptr %h)
  ret void
}

!llvm.module.flags = !{!0}
!0 = !{i32 1, !"bennett.dialect", !"0.1"}
```

from fractions import Fraction as Q

def dec(bits,p,e,bias):
 sign=-1 if bits>>(p-1+e) else 1
 exp=(bits>>(p-1))&((1<<e)-1)
 frac=bits&((1<<(p-1))-1)
 sig=frac+(1<<(p-1) if exp else 0)
 power=(exp or 1)-bias-p+1
 return sign*sig*(Q(2)**power)
u=Q(2)**-1074
h=dec(0x3ff0000000000001,53,11,1023)
H=dec(0x0030000000000001,53,11,1023)
v=dec(0x3c030000000000001200000000000000,113,15,16383)
assert h==1+Q(2)**-52
assert v==H+u/2
assert (h+u)*Q(1,2)+h*Q(1,2)==h+u/2
assert H>Q(2)**-1022
assert (h/u).denominator==(H/u).denominator==1
print('Independent rational decoding and exact FMA/conversion values: PASS')
print('Positive midpoint expected modes [nearest-even,down,up,toward-zero,nearest-away]: [L,L,U,L,U]; flags all 16')
print('Negated midpoint expected modes: [-L,-U,-L,-L,-U]; flags all 16')
# Exhaustively enumerate a small IEEE-style binary format (p=4, exponent=3),
# then canonical pairs; this is evidence about the rule, not a binary64 proof.
p,e,bias=4,3,3
vals={dec(b,p,e,bias):b for b in range(1<<(p+e)) if ((b>>(p-1))&((1<<e)-1)) "'!= (1<<e)-1}
vals[Q(0)]=0
order=sorted(vals)
def rne(x):
 dist=min(abs(v-x) for v in order)
 near=[v for v in order if abs(v-x)==dist]
 return min(near,key=lambda v:vals[v]&1)
pairs={}
for h0 in order:
 for l0 in order:
  s=h0+l0
  if s<order[0] or s>order[-1]: continue
  if rne(s)==h0:
   assert s not in pairs
   pairs[s]=(h0,l0)
sums=sorted(pairs)
fallback=[]
for L,U in zip(sums,sums[1:]):
 a,b=pairs[L],pairs[U]
 ix=1 if a[1] or b[1] else 0
 parity=(vals[a[ix]]&1,vals[b[ix]]&1)
 if parity[0]==parity[1]: fallback.append((a,b,parity))
 assert abs(L)!=abs(U) or L==U
print('"'Toy-format canonical pairs:',len(pairs),'adjacent intervals:',len(sums)-1,'equal-parity intervals:',len(fallback))
print('Toy check is supplementary evidence only, not a proof over binary64.')

### 6.6 Host–device boundary

A device entry's parameters and results of handle type bind to launch `QREG` arguments and
results (ISA §10). A `ptr` parameter of a device entry is a **handle parameter** iff its
candidate atom `ROOT(E, i)` (added by PT11 to every pointer parameter of every device entry)
reaches a marker handle operand; otherwise the candidate atom is removed before §6.2 is checked.
A handle parameter binds to `QREG`; host code may copy, store and compare launch register ids and pass
them back. Leak checking happens at program end and at `bennett_qreg_free`, not at each
device-entry return, which gives the REPL's lexical lifetime (NS §5; audit B-30). *(non-normative)*
A register returned to the host stays live across launches until the host frees it or a
`measure` consumes it (ISA §10: "register ids created by the run remain valid unless measured
or freed").

### 6.7 Copy, XOR and in-place rules
### 8.5 Entry state

**Reference semantics.** The region function runs on a **private copy of the caller's whole
classical memory at the call**, with the bound inputs; its writes are discarded except through
`OUT`, `tgt` and the bound device outputs (NS §4.2: "every other write being restored
workspace"). Constant globals, and mutable globals that only the oracle's own L closure writes,
equal their initialisers at every call.

**Realisation on ISA 0.8.** Let R be the abstract objects the oracle closure may load (§5.4),
minus constant globals and mutable globals written by no function outside the L closure.
Translate source read-set objects after normalisation to runtime backing allocations. If
R includes a caller alloca/frame, include its live shadow-stack backing storage and metadata,
bytes and addresses. If reachability is uncertain, include all live caller heap/stack backing
allocations. Whenever the L closure uses the shadow stack, add its state globals to G and
its block to Hb with current base, size and stack pointer, even without a source heap object.
Include the logical frame/allocation table of §17.3, dynamic extents, liveness and
save/restore state in G whenever stack access or stack operations can execute. Replay
these bytes before private allocations/accesses and let ss_init adopt them; a live
backing entry alone does not recreate live program frames. All such metadata mutations
invalidate snapshot reuse independently of the byte read set.
Allocator metadata is an independent entry-state dependency: allocation, deallocation,
allocation-validity checks and observation of allocation-derived addresses read it even
without an LLVM load. Replay the complete caller allocation history (sizes and live bits)
whenever any can execute, including when the byte read set R is empty. Bytes are included
only for byte dependencies; the sound default is the complete table, with a smaller proven
dependency optional. Only when both byte and allocator-state dependencies are absent may
transfer be omitted and globals start at their images
(ISA §3.7), as in rung 4 route A (SQLite §5.2(i)). After replay heap_used, atop and stack-state
values equal the caller's call-entry values.
Otherwise the plugin emits an **entry-state transfer**:

1. **Snapshot.** At the call the caller's tier forms the byte string
   S = k ‖ (size_i ‖ live_i)_{i<k} ‖ G ‖ Hb, where k is an 8-byte little-endian count of heap allocations in the
   caller's allocation table (in allocation order, ISA §7.1), `size_i` and `live_i` are 8-byte
   little-endian fields (`live_i` is 0 or 1), G is the bytes of the mutable globals in R in `layout.globals` order,
   and Hb is the bytes of every live heap allocation in allocation order if R contains a heap
   object or shadow-stack backing allocation (else empty); each live Hb segment has exactly size_i bytes, even for size_i=0. S is zero-padded to 2^A_s bytes, where A_s is the smallest exponent with
   2^A_s ≥ 8 + 16·N_alloc + Σ|G| + `heap_limit_max` (N_alloc the allocation-table capacity of
   the layout, §19.5); A_s is static.
   The snapshot bytes S are assembled in a plugin-added unnamed CC global of 2^A_s bytes,
   zero-filled before every build; thus all 16-byte leaf loads, including padding, are valid.
2. **Register.** The CC tier builds a register of 8·2^A_s wires holding S with a **concat
   tree** of A_s − 3 static-width CC functions `B_j` (j = 4, …, A_s): `B_4(p)` executes
   `qreg.new any 128`, loads the 16 bytes at p as two `i64` values and makes the `oracle_call`
   of the runtime oracle `@xor128` (§16.5) that XORs them into the register (with a token minted
   at launch for budget (6, 0, 0, 0) and a fresh 4-wire fault register, which stays clean and is
   freed); `B_j(p)` (j > 4)
   returns `qreg.concat B_{j−1}(p) B_{j−1}(p + 2^{j−1})`. Every type is static and the cost is
   2^(A_s−4) region calls. The register is bound as an extra `in` region `s0:region[A_s,8]`.
3. **Replay.** The wrapper prologue (§16.2) calls the runtimes function
   `__bennett_rt_entry_replay(s0)`, which for i < k executes `__bennett_nz_alloc(size_i)` (ISA
   `lift.alloc`) and, if `live_i` = 0, `__bennett_nz_free` of the result. ISA §4.2 `lift.alloc`
   and ISA §7.1 `alloc` share the rule "bump + max(n, 1) from `heap_base`", so the replay
   reproduces the caller's heap addresses exactly (audit O-07). It then copies G into the
   globals (by the driver-generated table `__bennett_rt_entry_table` of their addresses and
   sizes) and Hb into the replayed live allocations.
4. **Release.** The snapshot register holds the basis state |S⟩ and is unentangled after every
   oracle call (an `in` region is unchanged on every basis configuration, ISA §5.2). The caller
   keeps it for later calls only while its dependent bytes and allocator metadata are unchanged. Relevant alloc/free
   and shadow-stack push/pop/restore invalidate it as well as stores to R,
   and releases it by unpreparation: retain the classical S for the register's lifetime,
   take successive slice views of at most 128 bits, invoke the same @xor128 preparation
   oracle with the original two i64 arguments on each 128-bit chunk (one view per call),
   then qreg.free the now-clean whole register. A_s ≥ 4, so every leaf is exactly 128 bits.
   No whole-register measurement, view measurement or un-concat operation is needed.

The replay's rows, allocations and calls are part of the reference body and count against the
budget (§9.4). Entry-state transfer from R-tier code needs registers allocated in R (BLK-4,
§25.1); no rung uses it. **Build-time snapshots** (rung 7, §19.6) rewrite the initialisers of
the mutable globals to snapshot values at link time, so byte transfer may be empty; allocator dependencies still require replay.
Efficiency: G-12 (§25.3).

### 8.6 Oracle invocations and wide outputs
### 19.4 Placement: host vs device CC

H functions stay native unless they share **pointer-rich** memory with device code: a pointer
argument of a device-entry call, or a global accessed by both, whose pointee may contain pointers
that device code reads (§5). Such H functions are **promoted** to CC variants, transitively up to
their roots (in the limit `main`, and then H is the system-call proxy only: `hostcall
bennett.syscall`). Results are equal by the materialised semantics; ISA-11 would let them stay
native (an efficiency and placement item, §25.3; outline E11).

### 19.5 Layout: the comparison link

The driver links the materialised module natively (§17.7) with a linker script that fixes the
addresses: non-PIE; identical-code folding and constant merging off; the null region [0,
`null_size`) with `null_size` = 4096; the data sections (`.rodata`, `.data`, `.bss`, in that order)
from `data_base` = 4096; the text from `text_base` = 0x400000, or, when the device ranges below
(data, heap, arena, interface regions, computed first) end above 0x400000, from the first multiple
of 0x200000 at or after their end, so that the native comparison binary maps its code disjointly
from them; the defaults are options (`--bennett-layout-data-base`, `--bennett-layout-text-base`,
§24). The link map gives A(F) for
every function and the base of every global. The device `layout` (ISA §9.1) replicates it:

- `globals`: every global of the link at its link address with its image;
- `heap_base`: the first multiple of 4096 at or after the end of the data; `heap_limit_max`: the
  largest `heap` of the budget table (which covers the shadow stack, §9.2);
- the arena (TDL-4) at the first multiple of its size 2^A at or after the heap's end, with 2^A the
  smallest power of two covering the may-Co globals and the coherent allocator's heap (its size
  from `--bennett-arena-heap`, default the largest `heap` of the table); the arena's shadow
  `__bennett_rt_shadow` is a global of the data sections;
- interface regions per oracle above the arena (§16.3); H, A_tab and S_cells by §16.3;
- the production host image is linked with the same addresses for every function and global
  whose address may cross the boundary (§4.4).

*(non-normative)* With data low and text high, H covers the data, heap and regions; function
addresses are values only and never occupy device memory.

**Native layout ABI v1.** The driver MUST define exactly one external constant `@__bennett_rt_native_layout = constant { i64, i64, i64 } { i64 1, i64 heap_base, i64 heap_limit_max }, align 8` in comparison-only support, with fields at byte offsets 0, 8 and 16 in target little-endian order and values from this same resolved §19.5 layout; runtimes declares that object and validates the version/range before use, with no weak/default definition. The third field is a byte capacity, not an end address; it includes shadow-stack allocations under §9.2. This object has no relationship to blockaddress identity objects `__bennett_rt_blk.<f>`.

### 19.6 Build-time snapshots
### 17.7 The native comparison build

Differential testing (NS §5.1) compares the artefact with the **native comparison binary**: the
materialised device variants, code-generated by the same LLVM major and linked with
`bennett-runtimes` and `bennett-arith` (never host libc or libm) under the comparison link of
§19.5. It is never an `-O0` build: optimisation legally changes marker operands (spike F4), and
the materialised optimised module is the one program both builds implement. The driver MUST make
division by zero and INT_MIN/−1 trap in the native build: on targets whose divide instruction does
not trap it inserts, after materialisation and outside the materialised module, a check that
calls `__bennett_nz_fault` with code 5 or 6. Results compare by the outcome table of §18.7.

**Comparison-only support exception.** Only after the materialised device module has been fixed, the native comparison backend MAY define the three `__bennett_nz_` primitives of §3.1 as native support, and the driver MUST emit `__bennett_rt_native_layout` as specified in §19.5; these definitions MUST NOT enter producer inputs, production device bitcode, region identity or the materialised module used by the ISA map.

The native primitive support MUST reproduce the resolved device addresses, zero fill, live allocation sizes, nonreclaiming allocation/free history and fault outcomes of §§17.1/17.5, with capacity for every history admitted by the resolved heap budget (at most one heap record per byte since max(n,1) ≥ 1), without an arbitrary smaller record cap; host setup/resource failure MUST terminate comparison with an explicit infrastructure failure, never silently relocate the heap, choose defaults, use host malloc, or report a successful dialect outcome.

**Definedness classifiers.** The comparison binary MUST detect the materialised memory faults of §17.5 (out-of-live-allocation access, use after free and invalid/double free), division by zero and signed division overflow, and reached explicit fault/unreachable paths; instrumentation or a checked memory adapter may supply these checks outside the materialised module, and UBSan-only coverage MUST NOT be reported as satisfying the memory-fault obligation.

The harness MUST separately classify source inputs using instrumented original-input builds or a checked reference interpreter as in NS §3.2, report flagged inputs as undefined/excluded, and record the classifier's actual detection coverage; no classifier claims source UB erased by optimisation is detectable in the comparison binary.
